Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, I guess the main reason is that I feel like I’m not handling basic adult life very well, even things that seem like they should be simple. I keep forgetting important stuff—like paying bills on time, or I’ll mean to reply to an interview email and then somehow days go by and I realize I never answered it. I’ve missed appointments because I either forgot completely or remembered at the wrong time, and errands just kind of… don’t get finished. Like I’ll start the process of doing something and then it disappears from my brain until there’s a consequence.

And it’s not just annoying anymore. It’s starting to actually cost me things—opportunities, money, people’s trust, I guess. The interview emails especially have been really upsetting because I’m not working right now, and I know I need to be on top of those, but then I still don’t follow through. So that’s what finally made me think I should come in, because I don’t feel like I can just keep telling myself I’ll do better next time.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… emotionally, it’s kind of a mix, and I don’t always know how to separate it out neatly. In the moment when I realize I forgot something, there’s usually this really sharp drop in my stomach, like, “Oh no, not again.” Then I get embarrassed, especially if another person was depending on me or waiting for me to respond. I start thinking about how it must look from the outside, like I don’t care or I’m being irresponsible, even though that’s not how it feels from inside my head.

And then after that I get really frustrated with myself. Like, I’ll tell myself, “You’re 22, you graduated college, you should be able to answer an email or show up to something you scheduled.” It makes me feel pretty ashamed, honestly. Not every single time in some dramatic way, but it builds up. Each missed thing feels like evidence that I can’t trust myself, if that makes sense.

Sometimes I also get anxious afterward because I know there might be consequences, like someone being upset with me or me losing an opportunity. And then I’ll avoid looking at the email or the missed call for a while because I’m already dreading what it says, which probably makes it worse. But mostly it’s embarrassment, guilt, and this feeling of being really disappointed in myself.

Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that makes me feel the most confused about myself, because it’s not like I’m not trying or I don’t understand what I’m supposed to be doing. Like when I was still doing biotech data work, I would genuinely think I had finished something carefully — like a script or a spreadsheet — and I’d feel pretty confident it was ready. Then later I’d find out I had mislabeled a sample ID, or skipped over some note in the protocol that I should have included, or submitted documentation that had really obvious typos in it.

And the worst part is that some of the mistakes were not subtle. It would be something where, looking at it afterward, I’d be like, “How did I not see that?” I could stare at it while I was doing it and somehow still miss the thing right in front of me. It made me really nervous about trusting my own work, because I’d think I had checked it, but apparently my checking didn’t always mean I actually caught things.

But mostly it’s embarrassment, guilt, and this feeling of being really disappointed in myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or pay attention to details in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that makes me feel the most confused about myself, because it’s not like I’m not trying or I don’t understand what I’m supposed to be doing. Like when I was still doing biotech data work, I would genuinely think I had finished something carefully — like a script or a spreadsheet — and I’d feel pretty confident it was ready. Then later I’d find out I had mislabeled a sample ID, or skipped over some note in the protocol that I should have included, or submitted documentation that had really obvious typos in it.\n\nAnd the worst part is that some of the mistakes were not subtle. It would be something where, looking at it afterward, I’d be like, “How did I not see that?” I could stare at it while I was doing it and somehow still miss the thing right in front of me. It made me really nervous about trusting my own work, because I’d think I had checked it, but apparently my checking didn’t always mean I actually caught things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it started suddenly. It’s more like… I’m only now realizing how long it’s been there, because the consequences are bigger now. When I think back, even in elementary school, teachers used to write comments like, “She’s bright,” or “She understands the material,” but then there would always be this other part about how I was constantly losing worksheets or not turning things in because I couldn’t find them.

I remember rushing through directions, too, like I’d start before I’d fully read what I was supposed to do and then make mistakes that weren’t because I didn’t know the answer. And I talked out of turn a lot. Not in a trying-to-be-rude way, more like the thought came into my head and just came out of my mouth before I stopped it.

My desk was also… honestly kind of a disaster. I’d have papers shoved everywhere, and then when the teacher asked for an assignment, I’d be digging around while everyone else had theirs out already. So I guess the forgetting and attention stuff didn’t come out of nowhere. It feels like it’s been a pattern since I was a kid, but now there’s less structure around me, so it’s much harder to hide or compensate for.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think so. It feels like it’s been pretty consistent, just… it looked different when I was younger because adults were kind of holding the structure around me.

Like even in elementary school, I remember teachers writing things along the lines of, “She’s bright,” or that I understood the material, but then in the same breath there’d be comments about me constantly losing worksheets or not having things to turn in because I couldn’t find them. I’d do the work and then somehow it would disappear into my desk or backpack, and then I’d be sitting there panicking while everyone else just handed theirs in.

I also rushed through directions a lot. I’d see the first part of an assignment and just start doing it before I’d actually read the whole thing, and then I’d make these avoidable mistakes. And I talked out of turn a lot, too — not because I was trying to interrupt or be disrespectful, but because the thought would pop into my head and I’d say it before really stopping myself.

And my desk was always a mess. Like, not just a little cluttered — it was messy enough that I genuinely couldn’t find assignments when the teacher asked for them. So yes, I don’t think this is something that suddenly appeared in adulthood. It’s more like it’s been there since I was a kid, but now that I’m older, the consequences feel bigger and there’s less built-in help keeping me on track.

It made me really nervous about trusting my own work, because I’d think I had checked it, but apparently my checking didn’t always mean I actually caught things.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems with forgetting and attention start suddenly or have they been gradually getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it started suddenly. It’s more like… I’m only now realizing how long it’s been there, because the consequences are bigger now. When I think back, even in elementary school, teachers used to write comments like, “She’s bright,” or “She understands the material,” but then there would always be this other part about how I was constantly losing worksheets or not turning things in because I couldn’t find them.\n\nI remember rushing through directions, too, like I’d start before I’d fully read what I was supposed to do and then make mistakes that weren’t because I didn’t know the answer. And I talked out of turn a lot. Not in a trying-to-be-rude way, more like the thought came into my head and just came out of my mouth before I stopped it.\n\nMy desk was also… honestly kind of a disaster. I’d have papers shoved everywhere, and then when the teacher asked for an assignment, I’d be digging around while everyone else had theirs out already. So I guess the forgetting and attention stuff didn’t come out of nowhere. It feels like it’s been a pattern since I was a kid, but now there’s less structure around me, so it’s much harder to hide or compensate for.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with forgetting and attention consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It feels like it’s been pretty consistent, just… it looked different when I was younger because adults were kind of holding the structure around me.\n\nLike even in elementary school, I remember teachers writing things along the lines of, “She’s bright,” or that I understood the material, but then in the same breath there’d be comments about me constantly losing worksheets or not having things to turn in because I couldn’t find them. I’d see the first part of an assignment and just start doing it before I’d actually read the whole thing, and then I’d make these avoidable mistakes. And I talked out of turn a lot, too — not because I was trying to interrupt or be disrespectful, but because the thought would pop into my head and I’d say it before really stopping myself.\n\nAnd my desk was always a mess. Yes, a lot. It’s not always like I’m literally jumping up and running around, but inside it feels really intense. If I have to sit through something like an interview, or a meeting, or even just a movie, I get this pressure in my body where I feel like I need to shift around or get up or do something with my hands.

It’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s almost like I’ll crawl out of my skin if I just sit there completely still. I’ll be changing positions, bouncing my leg, picking at something, fiddling with my sleeves or whatever is nearby. And I’m aware of it, which makes it kind of embarrassing, especially if I’m supposed to look calm and attentive. I can want to sit still, but my body just feels like it’s arguing with me the whole time.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess the main reason is that I feel like I’m not handling basic adult life very well, even things that seem like they should be simple. I keep forgetting important stuff—like paying bills on time, or I’ll mean to reply to an interview email and then somehow days go by and I realize I never answered it. I’ve missed appointments because I either forgot completely or remembered at the wrong time, and errands just kind of… don’t get finished. Like I’ll start the process of doing something and then it disappears from my brain until there’s a consequence.\n\nAnd it’s not just annoying anymore. It’s starting to actually cost me things—opportunities, money, people’s trust, I guess. The interview emails especially have been really upsetting because I’m not working right now, and I know I need to be on top of those, but then I still don’t follow through. So that’s what finally made me think I should come in, because I don’t feel like I can just keep telling myself I’ll do better next time.\n\nDoctor: How have you been feeling emotionally about these situations where you've missed appointments or forgotten important tasks?\n\nPatient: I mean… emotionally, it’s kind of a mix, and I don’t always know how to separate it out neatly. In the moment when I realize I forgot something, there’s usually this really sharp drop in my stomach, like, “Oh no, not again.” Then I get embarrassed, especially if another person was depending on me or waiting for me to respond. I start thinking about how it must look from the outside, like I don’t care or I’m being irresponsible, even though that’s not how it feels from inside my head.\n\nAnd then after that I get really frustrated with myself. Like, I’ll tell myself, “You’re 22, you graduated college, you should be able to answer an email or show up to something you scheduled.” It makes me feel pretty ashamed, honestly. Not every single time in some dramatic way, but it builds up. Each missed thing feels like evidence that I can’t trust myself, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I also get anxious afterward because I know there might be consequences, like someone being upset with me or me losing an opportunity. And then I’ll avoid looking at the email or the missed call for a while because I’m already dreading what it says, which probably makes it worse. But mostly it’s embarrassment, guilt, and this feeling of being really disappointed in myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or pay attention to details in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that makes me feel the most confused about myself, because it’s not like I’m not trying or I don’t understand what I’m supposed to be doing. Like when I was still doing biotech data work, I would genuinely think I had finished something carefully — like a script or a spreadsheet — and I’d feel pretty confident it was ready. Then later I’d find out I had mislabeled a sample ID, or skipped over some note in the protocol that I should have included, or submitted documentation that had really obvious typos in it.\n\nAnd the worst part is that some of the mistakes were not subtle. It would be something where, looking at it afterward, I’d be like, “How did I not see that?” I could stare at it while I was doing it and somehow still miss the thing right in front of me. It made me really nervous about trusting my own work, because I’d think I had checked it, but apparently my checking didn’t always mean I actually caught things.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems with forgetting and attention start suddenly or have they been gradually getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it started suddenly. It’s more like… I’m only now realizing how long it’s been there, because the consequences are bigger now. When I think back, even in elementary school, teachers used to write comments like, “She’s bright,” or “She understands the material,” but then there would always be this other part about how I was constantly losing worksheets or not turning things in because I couldn’t find them.\n\nI remember rushing through directions, too, like I’d start before I’d fully read what I was supposed to do and then make mistakes that weren’t because I didn’t know the answer. And I talked out of turn a lot. Not in a trying-to-be-rude way, more like the thought came into my head and just came out of my mouth before I stopped it.\n\nMy desk was also… honestly kind of a disaster. I’d have papers shoved everywhere, and then when the teacher asked for an assignment, I’d be digging around while everyone else had theirs out already. So I guess the forgetting and attention stuff didn’t come out of nowhere. It feels like it’s been a pattern since I was a kid, but now there’s less structure around me, so it’s much harder to hide or compensate for.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with forgetting and attention consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It feels like it’s been pretty consistent, just… it looked different when I was younger because adults were kind of holding the structure around me.\n\nLike even in elementary school, I remember teachers writing things along the lines of, “She’s bright,” or that I understood the material, but then in the same breath there’d be comments about me constantly losing worksheets or not having things to turn in because I couldn’t find them. I’d do the work and then somehow it would disappear into my desk or backpack, and then I’d be sitting there panicking while everyone else just handed theirs in.\n\nI also rushed through directions a lot. I’d see the first part of an assignment and just start doing it before I’d actually read the whole thing, and then I’d make these avoidable mistakes. And I talked out of turn a lot, too — not because I was trying to interrupt or be disrespectful, but because the thought would pop into my head and I’d say it before really stopping myself.\n\nAnd my desk was always a mess. Like, not just a little cluttered — it was messy enough that I genuinely couldn’t find assignments when the teacher asked for them. So yes, I don’t think this is something that suddenly appeared in adulthood. It’s more like it’s been there since I was a kid, but now that I’m older, the consequences feel bigger and there’s less built-in help keeping me on track.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, a lot. It’s not always like I’m literally jumping up and running around, but inside it feels really intense. If I have to sit through something like an interview, or a meeting, or even just a movie, I get this pressure in my body where I feel like I need to shift around or get up or do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s almost like I’ll crawl out of my skin if I just sit there completely still. I’ll be changing positions, bouncing my leg, picking at something, fiddling with my sleeves or whatever is nearby. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I think it has. Maybe not always in this dramatic obvious way, but it affects how much people can count on me, and that definitely spills into relationships.

Since I got fired, it’s been especially hard because I’ll tell myself I’m going to follow up after an interview, or send the email, or check back in, and then somehow I miss the window or I forget until it feels too late and embarrassing. And then I avoid it more because I feel ashamed. So even when friends or family are trying to be supportive, I can feel them getting frustrated, like, “Okay, but why didn’t you just do the thing?” And I don’t really have a good answer that doesn’t sound like an excuse.

I also had to step back from a volunteer project I really cared about, which felt awful. It wasn’t that I stopped caring — I cared a lot — but I couldn’t reliably keep up with the basic steps, like remembering follow-ups and staying organized enough to be useful. And that made me feel like I was letting people down.

So now I’m unemployed, and it feels like I can’t even reliably manage the normal steps to move my life forward. That’s hard on my family, I think, because they want to help but they also don’t really understand why I keep dropping things. And with friends, I pull back sometimes because I’m embarrassed about where my life is right now.

Not in a trying-to-be-rude way, more like the thought came into my head and just came out of my mouth before I stopped it.\n\nMy desk was also… honestly kind of a disaster. I’d have papers shoved everywhere, and then when the teacher asked for an assignment, I’d be digging around while everyone else had theirs out already. So I guess the forgetting and attention stuff didn’t come out of nowhere. It feels like it’s been a pattern since I was a kid, but now there’s less structure around me, so it’s much harder to hide or compensate for.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with forgetting and attention consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It feels like it’s been pretty consistent, just… it looked different when I was younger because adults were kind of holding the structure around me.\n\nLike even in elementary school, I remember teachers writing things along the lines of, “She’s bright,” or that I understood the material, but then in the same breath there’d be comments about me constantly losing worksheets or not having things to turn in because I couldn’t find them. I’d do the work and then somehow it would disappear into my desk or backpack, and then I’d be sitting there panicking while everyone else just handed theirs in.\n\nI also rushed through directions a lot. I’d see the first part of an assignment and just start doing it before I’d actually read the whole thing, and then I’d make these avoidable mistakes. And I talked out of turn a lot, too — not because I was trying to interrupt or be disrespectful, but because the thought would pop into my head and I’d say it before really stopping myself.\n\nAnd my desk was always a mess. Like, not just a little cluttered — it was messy enough that I genuinely couldn’t find assignments when the teacher asked for them. So yes, I don’t think this is something that suddenly appeared in adulthood. It’s more like it’s been there since I was a kid, but now that I’m older, the consequences feel bigger and there’s less built-in help keeping me on track.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, a lot. It’s not always like I’m literally jumping up and running around, but inside it feels really intense. If I have to sit through something like an interview, or a meeting, or even just a movie, I get this pressure in my body where I feel like I need to shift around or get up or do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s almost like I’ll crawl out of my skin if I just sit there completely still. I’ll be changing positions, bouncing my leg, picking at something, fiddling with my sleeves or whatever is nearby. And I’m aware of it, which makes it kind of embarrassing, especially if I’m supposed to look calm and attentive. I can want to sit still, but my body just feels like it’s arguing with me the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with forgetting, attention, and restlessness affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it has. Maybe not always in this dramatic obvious way, but it affects how much people can count on me, and that definitely spills into relationships.\n\nSince I got fired, it’s been especially hard because I’ll tell myself I’m going to follow up after an interview, or send the email, or check back in, and then somehow I miss the window or I forget until it feels too late and embarrassing. And then I avoid it more because I feel ashamed. So even when friends or family are trying to be supportive, I can feel them getting frustrated, like, “Okay, but why didn’t you just do the thing?” And I don’t really have a good answer that doesn’t sound like an excuse.\n\nI also had to step back from a volunteer project I really cared about, which felt awful. It wasn’t that I stopped caring — I cared a lot — but I couldn’t reliably keep up with the basic steps, like remembering follow-ups and staying organized enough to be useful. And that made me feel like I was letting people down.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and it feels like I can’t even reliably manage the normal steps to move my life forward. That’s hard on my family, I think, because they want to help but they also don’t really understand why I keep dropping things. And with friends, I pull back sometimes because I’m embarrassed about where my life is right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Ruled out F43.8 as the patient's symptoms do not align closely with the criteria for Other specified mental and behavioural disorders.", "is_final": false}

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Honestly, that’s the part that feels the most painful right now, because it’s not just “oh, I forgot where I put my keys” or something small. It’s affecting the basic structure of my life.

Since I got fired, I’ve been trying to apply and interview for jobs, and I’ll have these moments where I genuinely intend to follow up after an interview — like I know I’m supposed to send the email, I’ll even think about what I want to say — and then I just… don’t do it in time. Or I remember later and it feels too late and awkward, and then I get ashamed and avoid it. And then that obviously doesn’t help me get hired, so I’m still unemployed, and it becomes this cycle where I feel more and more stuck.

It also affected a volunteer project I really cared about. I didn’t stop caring about it at all, but I had to step back because I couldn’t keep up reliably with the follow-through and organization side of it. That was really upsetting because it made me feel like I was letting people down, and also like I can’t trust myself with things that matter to me.

So yes, it’s had a significant impact. I feel like I can’t reliably manage the basic steps of moving my life forward right now, even when I really want to.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess the main reason is that I feel like I’m not handling basic adult life very well, even things that seem like they should be simple. I keep forgetting important stuff—like paying bills on time, or I’ll mean to reply to an interview email and then somehow days go by and I realize I never answered it. I’ve missed appointments because I either forgot completely or remembered at the wrong time, and errands just kind of… don’t get finished. Like I’ll start the process of doing something and then it disappears from my brain until there’s a consequence.\n\nAnd it’s not just annoying anymore. It’s starting to actually cost me things—opportunities, money, people’s trust, I guess. The interview emails especially have been really upsetting because I’m not working right now, and I know I need to be on top of those, but then I still don’t follow through. So that’s what finally made me think I should come in, because I don’t feel like I can just keep telling myself I’ll do better next time.\n\nDoctor: How have you been feeling emotionally about these situations where you've missed appointments or forgotten important tasks?\n\nPatient: I mean… emotionally, it’s kind of a mix, and I don’t always know how to separate it out neatly. In the moment when I realize I forgot something, there’s usually this really sharp drop in my stomach, like, “Oh no, not again.” Then I get embarrassed, especially if another person was depending on me or waiting for me to respond. I start thinking about how it must look from the outside, like I don’t care or I’m being irresponsible, even though that’s not how it feels from inside my head.\n\nAnd then after that I get really frustrated with myself. Like, I’ll tell myself, “You’re 22, you graduated college, you should be able to answer an email or show up to something you scheduled.” It makes me feel pretty ashamed, honestly. Not every single time in some dramatic way, but it builds up. Each missed thing feels like evidence that I can’t trust myself, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I also get anxious afterward because I know there might be consequences, like someone being upset with me or me losing an opportunity. And then I’ll avoid looking at the email or the missed call for a while because I’m already dreading what it says, which probably makes it worse. But mostly it’s embarrassment, guilt, and this feeling of being really disappointed in myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or pay attention to details in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that makes me feel the most confused about myself, because it’s not like I’m not trying or I don’t understand what I’m supposed to be doing. Like when I was still doing biotech data work, I would genuinely think I had finished something carefully — like a script or a spreadsheet — and I’d feel pretty confident it was ready. Then later I’d find out I had mislabeled a sample ID, or skipped over some note in the protocol that I should have included, or submitted documentation that had really obvious typos in it.\n\nAnd the worst part is that some of the mistakes were not subtle. It would be something where, looking at it afterward, I’d be like, “How did I not see that?” I could stare at it while I was doing it and somehow still miss the thing right in front of me. It made me really nervous about trusting my own work, because I’d think I had checked it, but apparently my checking didn’t always mean I actually caught things.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems with forgetting and attention start suddenly or have they been gradually getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it started suddenly. It’s more like… I’m only now realizing how long it’s been there, because the consequences are bigger now. When I think back, even in elementary school, teachers used to write comments like, “She’s bright,” or “She understands the material,” but then there would always be this other part about how I was constantly losing worksheets or not turning things in because I couldn’t find them.\n\nI remember rushing through directions, too, like I’d start before I’d fully read what I was supposed to do and then make mistakes that weren’t because I didn’t know the answer. And I talked out of turn a lot. Not in a trying-to-be-rude way, more like the thought came into my head and just came out of my mouth before I stopped it.\n\nMy desk was also… honestly kind of a disaster. I’d have papers shoved everywhere, and then when the teacher asked for an assignment, I’d be digging around while everyone else had theirs out already. So I guess the forgetting and attention stuff didn’t come out of nowhere. It feels like it’s been a pattern since I was a kid, but now there’s less structure around me, so it’s much harder to hide or compensate for.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with forgetting and attention consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It feels like it’s been pretty consistent, just… it looked different when I was younger because adults were kind of holding the structure around me.\n\nLike even in elementary school, I remember teachers writing things along the lines of, “She’s bright,” or that I understood the material, but then in the same breath there’d be comments about me constantly losing worksheets or not having things to turn in because I couldn’t find them. I’d do the work and then somehow it would disappear into my desk or backpack, and then I’d be sitting there panicking while everyone else just handed theirs in.\n\nI also rushed through directions a lot. I’d see the first part of an assignment and just start doing it before I’d actually read the whole thing, and then I’d make these avoidable mistakes. And I talked out of turn a lot, too — not because I was trying to interrupt or be disrespectful, but because the thought would pop into my head and I’d say it before really stopping myself.\n\nAnd my desk was always a mess. Like, not just a little cluttered — it was messy enough that I genuinely couldn’t find assignments when the teacher asked for them. So yes, I don’t think this is something that suddenly appeared in adulthood. It’s more like it’s been there since I was a kid, but now that I’m older, the consequences feel bigger and there’s less built-in help keeping me on track.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, a lot. It’s not always like I’m literally jumping up and running around, but inside it feels really intense. If I have to sit through something like an interview, or a meeting, or even just a movie, I get this pressure in my body where I feel like I need to shift around or get up or do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s almost like I’ll crawl out of my skin if I just sit there completely still. I’ll be changing positions, bouncing my leg, picking at something, fiddling with my sleeves or whatever is nearby. And I’m aware of it, which makes it kind of embarrassing, especially if I’m supposed to look calm and attentive. I can want to sit still, but my body just feels like it’s arguing with me the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with forgetting, attention, and restlessness affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it has. Maybe not always in this dramatic obvious way, but it affects how much people can count on me, and that definitely spills into relationships.\n\nSince I got fired, it’s been especially hard because I’ll tell myself I’m going to follow up after an interview, or send the email, or check back in, and then somehow I miss the window or I forget until it feels too late and embarrassing. And then I avoid it more because I feel ashamed. So even when friends or family are trying to be supportive, I can feel them getting frustrated, like, “Okay, but why didn’t you just do the thing?” And I don’t really have a good answer that doesn’t sound like an excuse.\n\nI also had to step back from a volunteer project I really cared about, which felt awful. It wasn’t that I stopped caring — I cared a lot — but I couldn’t reliably keep up with the basic steps, like remembering follow-ups and staying organized enough to be useful. And that made me feel like I was letting people down.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and it feels like I can’t even reliably manage the normal steps to move my life forward. That’s hard on my family, I think, because they want to help but they also don’t really understand why I keep dropping things. And with friends, I pull back sometimes because I’m embarrassed about where my life is right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact of your forgetfulness and attention issues on your daily life, such as in work, school, or personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. Honestly, that’s the part that feels the most painful right now, because it’s not just “oh, I forgot where I put my keys” or something small. It’s affecting the basic structure of my life.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve been trying to apply and interview for jobs, and I’ll have these moments where I genuinely intend to follow up after an interview — like I know I’m supposed to send the email, I’ll even think about what I want to say — and then I just… don’t do it in time. manifestation: This has been seriously affecting me for about 1 year and 2 months, starting around when things fell apart at the biotech job and continuing after I was fired.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 22 / no_occupation / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S009\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, a lot. It’s not always like I’m literally jumping up and running around, but inside it feels really intense. If I have to sit through something like an interview, or a meeting, or even just a movie, I get this pressure in my body where I feel like I need to shift around or get up or do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s almost like I’ll crawl out of my skin if I just sit there completely still. I’ll be changing positions, bouncing my leg, picking at something, fiddling with my sleeves or whatever is nearby. And I’m aware of it, which makes it kind of embarrassing, especially if I’m supposed to look calm and attentive. I can want to sit still, but my body just feels like it’s arguing with me the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with forgetting, attention, and restlessness affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it has. Maybe not always in this dramatic obvious way, but it affects how much people can count on me, and that definitely spills into relationships.\n\nSince I got fired, it’s been especially hard because I’ll tell myself I’m going to follow up after an interview, or send the email, or check back in, and then somehow I miss the window or I forget until it feels too late and embarrassing. And then I avoid it more because I feel ashamed. So even when friends or family are trying to be supportive, I can feel them getting frustrated, like, “Okay, but why didn’t you just do the thing?” And I don’t really have a good answer that doesn’t sound like an excuse.\n\nI also had to step back from a volunteer project I really cared about, which felt awful. It wasn’t that I stopped caring — I cared a lot — but I couldn’t reliably keep up with the basic steps, like remembering follow-ups and staying organized enough to be useful. And that made me feel like I was letting people down.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and it feels like I can’t even reliably manage the normal steps to move my life forward. That’s hard on my family, I think, because they want to help but they also don’t really understand why I keep dropping things. And with friends, I pull back sometimes because I’m embarrassed about where my life is right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact of your forgetfulness and attention issues on your daily life, such as in work, school, or personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. Honestly, that’s the part that feels the most painful right now, because it’s not just “oh, I forgot where I put my keys” or something small. It’s affecting the basic structure of my life.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve been trying to apply and interview for jobs, and I’ll have these moments where I genuinely intend to follow up after an interview — like I know I’m supposed to send the email, I’ll even think about what I want to say — and then I just… don’t do it in time. Or I remember later and it feels too late and awkward, and then I get ashamed and avoid it. And then that obviously doesn’t help me get hired, so I’m still unemployed, and it becomes this cycle where I feel more and more stuck.\n\nIt also affected a volunteer project I really cared about. I didn’t stop caring about it at all, but I had to step back because I couldn’t keep up reliably with the follow-through and organization side of it. That was really upsetting because it made me feel like I was letting people down, and also like I can’t trust myself with things that matter to me.\n\nSo yes, it’s had a significant impact. I feel like I can’t reliably manage the basic steps of moving my life forward right now, even when I really want to.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think so. For me it shows up a lot in conversations, especially if I think I know where someone is going with what they’re saying. I’ll jump in before they’ve actually finished the question or the thought, and I’ll answer what I thought they were asking — and then a second later I realize, oh, that wasn’t actually the question, or I just cut them off.

It happens in interviews too, which is… not great. I’ll be trying so hard to sound engaged and quick, and then I kind of leap ahead. Like they start asking something and my brain fills in the rest, so I start answering before they’re done. Sometimes I can see on their face that I missed what they were really getting at, and then I feel embarrassed and try to correct myself, but by then I’ve already made it awkward.

I don’t usually mean to be rude. It’s more like there’s this pressure to respond immediately, like if I don’t say it right then I’ll lose the thought or miss my chance. But then the consequence is that I interrupt people or answer the wrong thing, and I can tell it affects how I come across.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess the main reason is that I feel like I’m not handling basic adult life very well, even things that seem like they should be simple. I keep forgetting important stuff—like paying bills on time, or I’ll mean to reply to an interview email and then somehow days go by and I realize I never answered it. I’ve missed appointments because I either forgot completely or remembered at the wrong time, and errands just kind of… don’t get finished. Like I’ll start the process of doing something and then it disappears from my brain until there’s a consequence.\n\nAnd it’s not just annoying anymore. It’s starting to actually cost me things—opportunities, money, people’s trust, I guess. The interview emails especially have been really upsetting because I’m not working right now, and I know I need to be on top of those, but then I still don’t follow through. So that’s what finally made me think I should come in, because I don’t feel like I can just keep telling myself I’ll do better next time.\n\nDoctor: How have you been feeling emotionally about these situations where you've missed appointments or forgotten important tasks?\n\nPatient: I mean… emotionally, it’s kind of a mix, and I don’t always know how to separate it out neatly. In the moment when I realize I forgot something, there’s usually this really sharp drop in my stomach, like, “Oh no, not again.” Then I get embarrassed, especially if another person was depending on me or waiting for me to respond. I start thinking about how it must look from the outside, like I don’t care or I’m being irresponsible, even though that’s not how it feels from inside my head.\n\nAnd then after that I get really frustrated with myself. Like, I’ll tell myself, “You’re 22, you graduated college, you should be able to answer an email or show up to something you scheduled.” It makes me feel pretty ashamed, honestly. Not every single time in some dramatic way, but it builds up. Each missed thing feels like evidence that I can’t trust myself, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I also get anxious afterward because I know there might be consequences, like someone being upset with me or me losing an opportunity. And then I’ll avoid looking at the email or the missed call for a while because I’m already dreading what it says, which probably makes it worse. But mostly it’s embarrassment, guilt, and this feeling of being really disappointed in myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or pay attention to details in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that makes me feel the most confused about myself, because it’s not like I’m not trying or I don’t understand what I’m supposed to be doing. Like when I was still doing biotech data work, I would genuinely think I had finished something carefully — like a script or a spreadsheet — and I’d feel pretty confident it was ready. Then later I’d find out I had mislabeled a sample ID, or skipped over some note in the protocol that I should have included, or submitted documentation that had really obvious typos in it.\n\nAnd the worst part is that some of the mistakes were not subtle. It would be something where, looking at it afterward, I’d be like, “How did I not see that?” I could stare at it while I was doing it and somehow still miss the thing right in front of me. It made me really nervous about trusting my own work, because I’d think I had checked it, but apparently my checking didn’t always mean I actually caught things.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems with forgetting and attention start suddenly or have they been gradually getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it started suddenly. It’s more like… I’m only now realizing how long it’s been there, because the consequences are bigger now. When I think back, even in elementary school, teachers used to write comments like, “She’s bright,” or “She understands the material,” but then there would always be this other part about how I was constantly losing worksheets or not turning things in because I couldn’t find them.\n\nI remember rushing through directions, too, like I’d start before I’d fully read what I was supposed to do and then make mistakes that weren’t because I didn’t know the answer. And I talked out of turn a lot. Not in a trying-to-be-rude way, more like the thought came into my head and just came out of my mouth before I stopped it.\n\nMy desk was also… honestly kind of a disaster. I’d have papers shoved everywhere, and then when the teacher asked for an assignment, I’d be digging around while everyone else had theirs out already. So I guess the forgetting and attention stuff didn’t come out of nowhere. It feels like it’s been a pattern since I was a kid, but now there’s less structure around me, so it’s much harder to hide or compensate for.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with forgetting and attention consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It feels like it’s been pretty consistent, just… it looked different when I was younger because adults were kind of holding the structure around me.\n\nLike even in elementary school, I remember teachers writing things along the lines of, “She’s bright,” or that I understood the material, but then in the same breath there’d be comments about me constantly losing worksheets or not having things to turn in because I couldn’t find them. I’d do the work and then somehow it would disappear into my desk or backpack, and then I’d be sitting there panicking while everyone else just handed theirs in.\n\nI also rushed through directions a lot. I’d see the first part of an assignment and just start doing it before I’d actually read the whole thing, and then I’d make these avoidable mistakes. And I talked out of turn a lot, too — not because I was trying to interrupt or be disrespectful, but because the thought would pop into my head and I’d say it before really stopping myself.\n\nAnd my desk was always a mess. Like, not just a little cluttered — it was messy enough that I genuinely couldn’t find assignments when the teacher asked for them. So yes, I don’t think this is something that suddenly appeared in adulthood. It’s more like it’s been there since I was a kid, but now that I’m older, the consequences feel bigger and there’s less built-in help keeping me on track.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, a lot. It feels like it’s been pretty consistent, just… it looked different when I was younger because adults were kind of holding the structure around me.\n\nLike even in elementary school, I remember teachers writing things along the lines of, “She’s bright,” or that I understood the material, but then in the same breath there’d be comments about me constantly losing worksheets or not having things to turn in because I couldn’t find them. I’d do the work and then somehow it would disappear into my desk or backpack, and then I’d be sitting there panicking while everyone else just handed theirs in.\n\nI also rushed through directions a lot. I’d see the first part of an assignment and just start doing it before I’d actually read the whole thing, and then I’d make these avoidable mistakes. And I talked out of turn a lot, too — not because I was trying to interrupt or be disrespectful, but because the thought would pop into my head and I’d say it before really stopping myself.\n\nAnd my desk was always a mess. Like, not just a little cluttered — it was messy enough that I genuinely couldn’t find assignments when the teacher asked for them. So yes, I don’t think this is something that suddenly appeared in adulthood. It’s more like it’s been there since I was a kid, but now that I’m older, the consequences feel bigger and there’s less built-in help keeping me on track.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, a lot. It’s not always like I’m literally jumping up and running around, but inside it feels really intense. If I have to sit through something like an interview, or a meeting, or even just a movie, I get this pressure in my body where I feel like I need to shift around or get up or do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s almost like I’ll crawl out of my skin if I just sit there completely still. I’ll be changing positions, bouncing my leg, picking at something, fiddling with my sleeves or whatever is nearby. And I’m aware of it, which makes it kind of embarrassing, especially if I’m supposed to look calm and attentive. I can want to sit still, but my body just feels like it’s arguing with me the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with forgetting, attention, and restlessness affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it has. Maybe not always in this dramatic obvious way, but it affects how much people can count on me, and that definitely spills into relationships.\n\nSince I got fired, it’s been especially hard because I’ll tell myself I’m going to follow up after an interview, or send the email, or check back in, and then somehow I miss the window or I forget until it feels too late and embarrassing. And then I avoid it more because I feel ashamed. So even when friends or family are trying to be supportive, I can feel them getting frustrated, like, “Okay, but why didn’t you just do the thing?” And I don’t really have a good answer that doesn’t sound like an excuse.\n\nI also had to step back from a volunteer project I really cared about, which felt awful. It wasn’t that I stopped caring — I cared a lot — but I couldn’t reliably keep up with the basic steps, like remembering follow-ups and staying organized enough to be useful. And that made me feel like I was letting people down.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and it feels like I can’t even reliably manage the normal steps to move my life forward. That’s hard on my family, I think, because they want to help but they also don’t really understand why I keep dropping things. And with friends, I pull back sometimes because I’m embarrassed about where my life is right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact of your forgetfulness and attention issues on your daily life, such as in work, school, or personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. Honestly, that’s the part that feels the most painful right now, because it’s not just “oh, I forgot where I put my keys” or something small. It’s affecting the basic structure of my life.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve been trying to apply and interview for jobs, and I’ll have these moments where I genuinely intend to follow up after an interview — like I know I’m supposed to send the email, I’ll even think about what I want to say — and then I just… don’t do it in time. Or I remember later and it feels too late and awkward, and then I get ashamed and avoid it. And then that obviously doesn’t help me get hired, so I’m still unemployed, and it becomes this cycle where I feel more and more stuck.\n\nIt also affected a volunteer project I really cared about. I didn’t stop caring about it at all, but I had to step back because I couldn’t keep up reliably with the follow-through and organization side of it. That was really upsetting because it made me feel like I was letting people down, and also like I can’t trust myself with things that matter to me.\n\nSo yes, it’s had a significant impact. I feel like I can’t reliably manage the basic steps of moving my life forward right now, even when I really want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. For me it shows up a lot in conversations, especially if I think I know where someone is going with what they’re saying. I’ll jump in before they’ve actually finished the question or the thought, and I’ll answer what I thought they were asking — and then a second later I realize, oh, that wasn’t actually the question, or I just cut them off.\n\nIt happens in interviews too, which is… not great. I’ll be trying so hard to sound engaged and quick, and then I kind of leap ahead. Like they start asking something and my brain fills in the rest, so I start answering before they’re done. Sometimes I can see on their face that I missed what they were really getting at, and then I feel embarrassed and try to correct myself, but by then I’ve already made it awkward.\n\nI don’t usually mean to be rude. It’s more like there’s this pressure to respond immediately, like if I don’t say it right then I’ll lose the thought or miss my chance. But then the consequence is that I interrupt people or answer the wrong thing, and I can tell it affects how I come across.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess the main reason is that I feel like I’m not handling basic adult life very well, even things that seem like they should be simple. I keep forgetting important stuff—like paying bills on time, or I’ll mean to reply to an interview email and then somehow days go by and I realize I never answered it. I’ve missed appointments because I either forgot completely or remembered at the wrong time, and errands just kind of… don’t get finished. Like I’ll start the process of doing something and then it disappears from my brain until there’s a consequence.\n\nAnd it’s not just annoying anymore. It’s starting to actually cost me things—opportunities, money, people’s trust, I guess. The interview emails especially have been really upsetting because I’m not working right now, and I know I need to be on top of those, but then I still don’t follow through. So that’s what finally made me think I should come in, because I don’t feel like I can just keep telling myself I’ll do better next time.\n\nDoctor: How have you been feeling emotionally about these situations where you've missed appointments or forgotten important tasks?\n\nPatient: I mean… emotionally, it’s kind of a mix, and I don’t always know how to separate it out neatly. In the moment when I realize I forgot something, there’s usually this really sharp drop in my stomach, like, “Oh no, not again.” Then I get embarrassed, especially if another person was depending on me or waiting for me to respond. I start thinking about how it must look from the outside, like I don’t care or I’m being irresponsible, even though that’s not how it feels from inside my head.\n\nAnd then after that I get really frustrated with myself. Like, I’ll tell myself, “You’re 22, you graduated college, you should be able to answer an email or show up to something you scheduled.” It makes me feel pretty ashamed, honestly. Not every single time in some dramatic way, but it builds up. Each missed thing feels like evidence that I can’t trust myself, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I also get anxious afterward because I know there might be consequences, like someone being upset with me or me losing an opportunity. And then I’ll avoid looking at the email or the missed call for a while because I’m already dreading what it says, which probably makes it worse. But mostly it’s embarrassment, guilt, and this feeling of being really disappointed in myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or pay attention to details in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that makes me feel the most confused about myself, because it’s not like I’m not trying or I don’t understand what I’m supposed to be doing. Like when I was still doing biotech data work, I would genuinely think I had finished something carefully — like a script or a spreadsheet — and I’d feel pretty confident it was ready. Then later I’d find out I had mislabeled a sample ID, or skipped over some note in the protocol that I should have included, or submitted documentation that had really obvious typos in it.\n\nAnd the worst part is that some of the mistakes were not subtle. It would be something where, looking at it afterward, I’d be like, “How did I not see that?” I could stare at it while I was doing it and somehow still miss the thing right in front of me. It made me really nervous about trusting my own work, because I’d think I had checked it, but apparently my checking didn’t always mean I actually caught things.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems with forgetting and attention start suddenly or have they been gradually getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it started suddenly. It’s more like… I’m only now realizing how long it’s been there, because the consequences are bigger now. When I think back, even in elementary school, teachers used to write comments like, “She’s bright,” or “She understands the material,” but then there would always be this other part about how I was constantly losing worksheets or not turning things in because I couldn’t find them.\n\nI remember rushing through directions, too, like I’d start before I’d fully read what I was supposed to do and then make mistakes that weren’t because I didn’t know the answer. And I talked out of turn a lot. Not in a trying-to-be-rude way, more like the thought came into my head and just came out of my mouth before I stopped it.\n\nMy desk was also… honestly kind of a disaster. I’d have papers shoved everywhere, and then when the teacher asked for an assignment, I’d be digging around while everyone else had theirs out already. So I guess the forgetting and attention stuff didn’t come out of nowhere. It feels like it’s been a pattern since I was a kid, but now there’s less structure around me, so it’s much harder to hide or compensate for.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with forgetting and attention consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It feels like it’s been pretty consistent, just… it looked different when I was younger because adults were kind of holding the structure around me.\n\nLike even in elementary school, I remember teachers writing things along the lines of, “She’s bright,” or that I understood the material, but then in the same breath there’d be comments about me constantly losing worksheets or not having things to turn in because I couldn’t find them. I’d do the work and then somehow it would disappear into my desk or backpack, and then I’d be sitting there panicking while everyone else just handed theirs in.\n\nI also rushed through directions a lot. I’d see the first part of an assignment and just start doing it before I’d actually read the whole thing, and then I’d make these avoidable mistakes. And I talked out of turn a lot, too — not because I was trying to interrupt or be disrespectful, but because the thought would pop into my head and I’d say it before really stopping myself.\n\nAnd my desk was always a mess. Like, not just a little cluttered — it was messy enough that I genuinely couldn’t find assignments when the teacher asked for them. So yes, I don’t think this is something that suddenly appeared in adulthood. It’s more like it’s been there since I was a kid, but now that I’m older, the consequences feel bigger and there’s less built-in help keeping me on track.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, a lot. It’s not always like I’m literally jumping up and running around, but inside it feels really intense. If I have to sit through something like an interview, or a meeting, or even just a movie, I get this pressure in my body where I feel like I need to shift around or get up or do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s almost like I’ll crawl out of my skin if I just sit there completely still. I’ll be changing positions, bouncing my leg, picking at something, fiddling with my sleeves or whatever is nearby. And I’m aware of it, which makes it kind of embarrassing, especially if I’m supposed to look calm and attentive. I can want to sit still, but my body just feels like it’s arguing with me the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with forgetting, attention, and restlessness affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it has. Maybe not always in this dramatic obvious way, but it affects how much people can count on me, and that definitely spills into relationships.\n\nSince I got fired, it’s been especially hard because I’ll tell myself I’m going to follow up after an interview, or send the email, or check back in, and then somehow I miss the window or I forget until it feels too late and embarrassing. And then I avoid it more because I feel ashamed. So even when friends or family are trying to be supportive, I can feel them getting frustrated, like, “Okay, but why didn’t you just do the thing?” And I don’t really have a good answer that doesn’t sound like an excuse.\n\nI also had to step back from a volunteer project I really cared about, which felt awful. It wasn’t that I stopped caring — I cared a lot — but I couldn’t reliably keep up with the basic steps, like remembering follow-ups and staying organized enough to be useful. And that made me feel like I was letting people down.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and it feels like I can’t even reliably manage the normal steps to move my life forward. That’s hard on my family, I think, because they want to help but they also don’t really understand why I keep dropping things. And with friends, I pull back sometimes because I’m embarrassed about where my life is right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact of your forgetfulness and attention issues on your daily life, such as in work, school, or personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. Honestly, that’s the part that feels the most painful right now, because it’s not just “oh, I forgot where I put my keys” or something small. It’s affecting the basic structure of my life.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve been trying to apply and interview for jobs, and I’ll have these moments where I genuinely intend to follow up after an interview — like I know I’m supposed to send the email, I’ll even think about what I want to say — and then I just… don’t do it in time. Or I remember later and it feels too late and awkward, and then I get ashamed and avoid it. And then that obviously doesn’t help me get hired, so I’m still unemployed, and it becomes this cycle where I feel more and more stuck.\n\nIt also affected a volunteer project I really cared about. I didn’t stop caring about it at all, but I had to step back because I couldn’t keep up reliably with the follow-through and organization side of it. That was really upsetting because it made me feel like I was letting people down, and also like I can’t trust myself with things that matter to me.\n\nSo yes, it’s had a significant impact. I feel like I can’t reliably manage the basic steps of moving my life forward right now, even when I really want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. For me it shows up a lot in conversations, especially if I think I know where someone is going with what they’re saying. I’ll jump in before they’ve actually finished the question or the thought, and I’ll answer what I thought they were asking — and then a second later I realize, oh, that wasn’t actually the question, or I just cut them off.\n\nIt happens in interviews too, which is… not great. I’ll be trying so hard to sound engaged and quick, and then I kind of leap ahead. Like they start asking something and my brain fills in the rest, so I start answering before they’re done. Sometimes I can see on their face that I missed what they were really getting at, and then I feel embarrassed and try to correct myself, but by then I’ve already made it awkward.\n\nI don’t usually mean to be rude. It’s more like there’s this pressure to respond immediately, like if I don’t say it right then I’ll lose the thought or miss my chance. But then the consequence is that I interrupt people or answer the wrong thing, and I can tell it affects how I come across.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or considered having ADHD?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I’m here because I’ve wondered if there’s some kind of explanation for why I keep struggling with things that seem like they should be basic, but I haven’t had some clear, formal answer before this.\n\nI guess I’ve considered that maybe there’s something going on, because it feels bigger than just being lazy or careless. I mostly just know that the problems have been real and they’ve been affecting my life a lot, and I’m trying to understand why.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

Honestly, that’s the part that feels the most painful right now, because it’s not just “oh, I forgot where I put my keys” or something small. It’s affecting the basic structure of my life.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve been trying to apply and interview for jobs, and I’ll have these moments where I genuinely intend to follow up after an interview — like I know I’m supposed to send the email, I’ll even think about what I want to say — and then I just… don’t do it in time. Or I remember later and it feels too late and awkward, and then I get ashamed and avoid it. And then that obviously doesn’t help me get hired, so I’m still unemployed, and it becomes this cycle where I feel more and more stuck.\n\nIt also affected a volunteer project I really cared about. I didn’t stop caring about it at all, but I had to step back because I couldn’t keep up reliably with the follow-through and organization side of it. That was really upsetting because it made me feel like I was letting people down, and also like I can’t trust myself with things that matter to me.\n\nSo yes, it’s had a significant impact. I feel like I can’t reliably manage the basic steps of moving my life forward right now, even when I really want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. For me it shows up a lot in conversations, especially if I think I know where someone is going with what they’re saying. I’ll jump in before they’ve actually finished the question or the thought, and I’ll answer what I thought they were asking — and then a second later I realize, oh, that wasn’t actually the question, or I just cut them off.\n\nIt happens in interviews too, which is… not great. I’ll be trying so hard to sound engaged and quick, and then I kind of leap ahead. Like they start asking something and my brain fills in the rest, so I start answering before they’re done. Sometimes I can see on their face that I missed what they were really getting at, and then I feel embarrassed and try to correct myself, but by then I’ve already made it awkward.\n\nI don’t usually mean to be rude. It’s more like there’s this pressure to respond immediately, like if I don’t say it right then I’ll lose the thought or miss my chance. But then the consequence is that I interrupt people or answer the wrong thing, and I can tell it affects how I come across.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or considered having ADHD?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I’m here because I’ve wondered if there’s some kind of explanation for why I keep struggling with things that seem like they should be basic, but I haven’t had some clear, formal answer before this.\n\nI guess I’ve considered that maybe there’s something going on, because it feels bigger than just being lazy or careless. But I’m also hesitant to say, “Oh yes, it’s definitely that,” because I don’t want to diagnose myself or sound like I’m just grabbing onto a label. I mostly just know that the problems have been real and they’ve been affecting my life a lot, and I’m trying to understand why.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever find yourself so intensely focused on something that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 22 / no_occupation / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure. I don’t know if I’d describe it as being so intensely focused that the whole world disappears, if that’s what you mean.

I mean, I can get absorbed in something sometimes, like anyone can, especially if it’s interesting or if I feel pressure to finish it. But I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern where I regularly lose track of everything around me in a major way. It’s possible I do it and don’t label it that way, but nothing specific is coming to mind that feels important enough to say yes to.

When you say “lose track of everything else,” do you mean like forgetting to eat or missing appointments, or more like just being really mentally locked into something for a while?
