  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess the main reason is that I feel like I’m not handling basic adult life very well, even things that seem like they should be simple. I keep forgetting important stuff—like paying bills on time, or I’ll mean to reply to an interview email and then somehow days go by and I realize I never answered it. I’ve missed appointments because I either forgot completely or remembered at the wrong time, and errands just kind of… don’t get finished. Like I’ll start the process of doing something and then it disappears from my brain until there’s a consequence.\n\nAnd it’s not just annoying anymore. It’s starting to actually cost me things—opportunities, money, people’s trust, I guess. The interview emails especially have been really upsetting because I’m not working right now, and I know I need to be on top of those, but then I still don’t follow through. So that’s what finally made me think I should come in, because I don’t feel like I can just keep telling myself I’ll do better next time.\n\nDoctor: How have you been feeling emotionally about these situations where you've missed appointments or forgotten important tasks?\n\nPatient: I mean… emotionally, it’s kind of a mix, and I don’t always know how to separate it out neatly. In the moment when I realize I forgot something, there’s usually this really sharp drop in my stomach, like, “Oh no, not again.” Then I get embarrassed, especially if another person was depending on me or waiting for me to respond. I start thinking about how it must look from the outside, like I don’t care or I’m being irresponsible, even though that’s not how it feels from inside my head.\n\nAnd then after that I get really frustrated with myself. Like, I’ll tell myself, “You’re 22, you graduated college, you should be able to answer an email or show up to something you scheduled.” It makes me feel pretty ashamed, honestly. Not every single time in some dramatic way, but it builds up. Each missed thing feels like evidence that I can’t trust myself, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I also get anxious afterward because I know there might be consequences, like someone being upset with me or me losing an opportunity. And then I’ll avoid looking at the email or the missed call for a while because I’m already dreading what it says, which probably makes it worse. But mostly it’s embarrassment, guilt, and this feeling of being really disappointed in myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or pay attention to details in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that makes me feel the most confused about myself, because it’s not like I’m not trying or I don’t understand what I’m supposed to be doing. Like when I was still doing biotech data work, I would genuinely think I had finished something carefully — like a script or a spreadsheet — and I’d feel pretty confident it was ready. Then later I’d find out I had mislabeled a sample ID, or skipped over some note in the protocol that I should have included, or submitted documentation that had really obvious typos in it.\n\nAnd the worst part is that some of the mistakes were not subtle. It would be something where, looking at it afterward, I’d be like, “How did I not see that?” I could stare at it while I was doing it and somehow still miss the thing right in front of me. It made me really nervous about trusting my own work, because I’d think I had checked it, but apparently my checking didn’t always mean I actually caught things.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems with forgetting and attention start suddenly or have they been gradually getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it started suddenly. It’s more like… I’m only now realizing how long it’s been there, because the consequences are bigger now. When I think back, even in elementary school, teachers used to write comments like, “She’s bright,” or “She understands the material,” but then there would always be this other part about how I was constantly losing worksheets or not turning things in because I couldn’t find them.\n\nI remember rushing through directions, too, like I’d start before I’d fully read what I was supposed to do and then make mistakes that weren’t because I didn’t know the answer. And I talked out of turn a lot. Not in a trying-to-be-rude way, more like the thought came into my head and just came out of my mouth before I stopped it.\n\nMy desk was also… honestly kind of a disaster. I’d have papers shoved everywhere, and then when the teacher asked for an assignment, I’d be digging around while everyone else had theirs out already. So I guess the forgetting and attention stuff didn’t come out of nowhere. It feels like it’s been a pattern since I was a kid, but now there’s less structure around me, so it’s much harder to hide or compensate for.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with forgetting and attention consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It feels like it’s been pretty consistent, just… it looked different when I was younger because adults were kind of holding the structure around me.\n\nLike even in elementary school, I remember teachers writing things along the lines of, “She’s bright,” or that I understood the material, but then in the same breath there’d be comments about me constantly losing worksheets or not having things to turn in because I couldn’t find them. I’d do the work and then somehow it would disappear into my desk or backpack, and then I’d be sitting there panicking while everyone else just handed theirs in.\n\nI also rushed through directions a lot. I’d see the first part of an assignment and just start doing it before I’d actually read the whole thing, and then I’d make these avoidable mistakes. And I talked out of turn a lot, too — not because I was trying to interrupt or be disrespectful, but because the thought would pop into my head and I’d say it before really stopping myself.\n\nAnd my desk was always a mess. Like, not just a little cluttered — it was messy enough that I genuinely couldn’t find assignments when the teacher asked for them. So yes, I don’t think this is something that suddenly appeared in adulthood. It’s more like it’s been there since I was a kid, but now that I’m older, the consequences feel bigger and there’s less built-in help keeping me on track.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, a lot. It’s not always like I’m literally jumping up and running around, but inside it feels really intense. If I have to sit through something like an interview, or a meeting, or even just a movie, I get this pressure in my body where I feel like I need to shift around or get up or do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s almost like I’ll crawl out of my skin if I just sit there completely still. I’ll be changing positions, bouncing my leg, picking at something, fiddling with my sleeves or whatever is nearby. And I’m aware of it, which makes it kind of embarrassing, especially if I’m supposed to look calm and attentive. I can want to sit still, but my body just feels like it’s arguing with me the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with forgetting, attention, and restlessness affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it has. Maybe not always in this dramatic obvious way, but it affects how much people can count on me, and that definitely spills into relationships.\n\nSince I got fired, it’s been especially hard because I’ll tell myself I’m going to follow up after an interview, or send the email, or check back in, and then somehow I miss the window or I forget until it feels too late and embarrassing. And then I avoid it more because I feel ashamed. So even when friends or family are trying to be supportive, I can feel them getting frustrated, like, “Okay, but why didn’t you just do the thing?” And I don’t really have a good answer that doesn’t sound like an excuse.\n\nI also had to step back from a volunteer project I really cared about, which felt awful. It wasn’t that I stopped caring — I cared a lot — but I couldn’t reliably keep up with the basic steps, like remembering follow-ups and staying organized enough to be useful. And that made me feel like I was letting people down.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and it feels like I can’t even reliably manage the normal steps to move my life forward. That’s hard on my family, I think, because they want to help but they also don’t really understand why I keep dropping things. And with friends, I pull back sometimes because I’m embarrassed about where my life is right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact of your forgetfulness and attention issues on your daily life, such as in work, school, or personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. Honestly, that’s the part that feels the most painful right now, because it’s not just “oh, I forgot where I put my keys” or something small. It’s affecting the basic structure of my life.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve been trying to apply and interview for jobs, and I’ll have these moments where I genuinely intend to follow up after an interview — like I know I’m supposed to send the email, I’ll even think about what I want to say — and then I just… don’t do it in time. Or I remember later and it feels too late and awkward, and then I get ashamed and avoid it. And then that obviously doesn’t help me get hired, so I’m still unemployed, and it becomes this cycle where I feel more and more stuck.\n\nIt also affected a volunteer project I really cared about. I didn’t stop caring about it at all, but I had to step back because I couldn’t keep up reliably with the follow-through and organization side of it. That was really upsetting because it made me feel like I was letting people down, and also like I can’t trust myself with things that matter to me.\n\nSo yes, it’s had a significant impact. I feel like I can’t reliably manage the basic steps of moving my life forward right now, even when I really want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. For me it shows up a lot in conversations, especially if I think I know where someone is going with what they’re saying. I’ll jump in before they’ve actually finished the question or the thought, and I’ll answer what I thought they were asking — and then a second later I realize, oh, that wasn’t actually the question, or I just cut them off.\n\nIt happens in interviews too, which is… not great. I’ll be trying so hard to sound engaged and quick, and then I kind of leap ahead. Like they start asking something and my brain fills in the rest, so I start answering before they’re done. Sometimes I can see on their face that I missed what they were really getting at, and then I feel embarrassed and try to correct myself, but by then I’ve already made it awkward.\n\nI don’t usually mean to be rude. But I’m also hesitant to say, “Oh yes, it’s definitely that,” because I don’t want to diagnose myself or sound like I’m just grabbing onto a label. I mostly just know that the problems have been real and they’ve been affecting my life a lot, and I’m trying to understand why.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so intensely focused on something that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I don’t know if I’d describe it as being so intensely focused that the whole world disappears, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I can get absorbed in something sometimes, like anyone can, especially if it’s interesting or if I feel pressure to finish it. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Yes. Very much, actually. It’s one of those things I don’t always notice right away because it feels so normal to me, but if I’m in a situation where I’m supposed to just sit still and pay attention, it’s like there’s this pressure building inside my body.

Even watching a movie can be hard if I’m expected to just sit there quietly the whole time. It’s not always that I’m anxious exactly — sometimes it’s more physical, like I’m uncomfortable in my own skin.

I’ll cross and uncross my legs, mess with my fingers, adjust how I’m sitting, hold something if I can. If I can’t move at all, it feels almost unbearable, like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. I know that sounds dramatic, but that’s really what it feels like sometimes.

I mostly just know that the problems have been real and they’ve been affecting my life a lot, and I’m trying to understand why.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so intensely focused on something that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I don’t know if I’d describe it as being so intensely focused that the whole world disappears, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I can get absorbed in something sometimes, like anyone can, especially if it’s interesting or if I feel pressure to finish it. But I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern where I regularly lose track of everything around me in a major way. Very much, actually. It’s not always that I’m anxious exactly — sometimes it’s more physical, like I’m uncomfortable in my own skin.\n\nI’ll cross and uncross my legs, mess with my fingers, adjust how I’m sitting, hold something if I can. It feels like it’s been pretty consistent, just… it looked different when I was younger because adults were kind of holding the structure around me.\n\nLike even in elementary school, I remember teachers writing things along the lines of, “She’s bright,” or that I understood the material, but then in the same breath there’d be comments about me constantly losing worksheets or not having things to turn in because I couldn’t find them. I’d do the work and then somehow it would disappear into my desk or backpack, and then I’d be sitting there panicking while everyone else just handed theirs in.\n\nI also rushed through directions a lot. I’d see the first part of an assignment and just start doing it before I’d actually read the whole thing, and then I’d make these avoidable mistakes. Like, not just a little cluttered — it was messy enough that I genuinely couldn’t find assignments when the teacher asked for them. So yes, I don’t think this is something that suddenly appeared in adulthood. It’s more like it’s been there since I was a kid, but now that I’m older, the consequences feel bigger and there’s less built-in help keeping me on track.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, a lot. It’s not always like I’m literally jumping up and running around, but inside it feels really intense. If I have to sit through something like an interview, or a meeting, or even just a movie, I get this pressure in my body where I feel like I need to shift around or get up or do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s almost like I’ll crawl out of my skin if I just sit there completely still. I’ll be changing positions, bouncing my leg, picking at something, fiddling with my sleeves or whatever is nearby. So even when friends or family are trying to be supportive, I can feel them getting frustrated, like, “Okay, but why didn’t you just do the thing?” And I don’t really have a good answer that doesn’t sound like an excuse.\n\nI also had to step back from a volunteer project I really cared about, which felt awful. I know that sounds dramatic, but that’s really what it feels like sometimes.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your difficulties with attention, memory, and restlessness significantly impacted your ability to maintain a job or achieve your educational goals?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 22 / no_occupation / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"Since getting fired, I have missed interview follow-ups and had to step back from a volunteer project I cared about, so I am unemployed and feel like I cannot reliably manage the basic steps of moving my life forward.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean… unfortunately, yes, it’s affected that pretty significantly. Since I got fired, it’s felt like I can’t even reliably manage the steps that come after that, if that makes sense.

Like, it’s not just “I don’t have a job right now.” It’s that I’ll have an interview or a possible opportunity, and then I miss the follow-up email, or I forget to respond soon enough, or I get overwhelmed by the basic next step and then too much time passes. And then I feel awful because from the outside it probably looks like I don’t care or I’m not serious, but I do care. I just keep dropping the ball at exactly the moments where I’m supposed to be proving I can handle things.

I also had to step back from a volunteer project that I actually cared about, which was really upsetting. It wasn’t that I stopped caring about it — I really did care — but I couldn’t keep up in a dependable way. And that’s kind of the theme: I feel like I’m not dependable, even when I want to be.

So now I’m unemployed, and it feels like I can’t reliably manage the basic things I’m supposed to do to move my life forward. Applying, following up, staying on top of commitments, keeping momentum — those things feel much harder than they seem like they should be. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I have a degree, and I feel like I should be able to function better than this.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess the main reason is that I feel like I’m not handling basic adult life very well, even things that seem like they should be simple. I keep forgetting important stuff—like paying bills on time, or I’ll mean to reply to an interview email and then somehow days go by and I realize I never answered it. I’ve missed appointments because I either forgot completely or remembered at the wrong time, and errands just kind of… don’t get finished. Like I’ll start the process of doing something and then it disappears from my brain until there’s a consequence.\n\nAnd it’s not just annoying anymore. It’s starting to actually cost me things—opportunities, money, people’s trust, I guess. The interview emails especially have been really upsetting because I’m not working right now, and I know I need to be on top of those, but then I still don’t follow through. So that’s what finally made me think I should come in, because I don’t feel like I can just keep telling myself I’ll do better next time.\n\nDoctor: How have you been feeling emotionally about these situations where you've missed appointments or forgotten important tasks?\n\nPatient: I mean… emotionally, it’s kind of a mix, and I don’t always know how to separate it out neatly. In the moment when I realize I forgot something, there’s usually this really sharp drop in my stomach, like, “Oh no, not again.” Then I get embarrassed, especially if another person was depending on me or waiting for me to respond. I start thinking about how it must look from the outside, like I don’t care or I’m being irresponsible, even though that’s not how it feels from inside my head.\n\nAnd then after that I get really frustrated with myself. Like, I’ll tell myself, “You’re 22, you graduated college, you should be able to answer an email or show up to something you scheduled.” It makes me feel pretty ashamed, honestly. Not every single time in some dramatic way, but it builds up. Each missed thing feels like evidence that I can’t trust myself, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I also get anxious afterward because I know there might be consequences, like someone being upset with me or me losing an opportunity. And then I’ll avoid looking at the email or the missed call for a while because I’m already dreading what it says, which probably makes it worse. But mostly it’s embarrassment, guilt, and this feeling of being really disappointed in myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or pay attention to details in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that makes me feel the most confused about myself, because it’s not like I’m not trying or I don’t understand what I’m supposed to be doing. Like when I was still doing biotech data work, I would genuinely think I had finished something carefully — like a script or a spreadsheet — and I’d feel pretty confident it was ready. Then later I’d find out I had mislabeled a sample ID, or skipped over some note in the protocol that I should have included, or submitted documentation that had really obvious typos in it.\n\nAnd the worst part is that some of the mistakes were not subtle. It would be something where, looking at it afterward, I’d be like, “How did I not see that?” I could stare at it while I was doing it and somehow still miss the thing right in front of me. It made me really nervous about trusting my own work, because I’d think I had checked it, but apparently my checking didn’t always mean I actually caught things.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems with forgetting and attention start suddenly or have they been gradually getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it started suddenly. It’s more like… I’m only now realizing how long it’s been there, because the consequences are bigger now. When I think back, even in elementary school, teachers used to write comments like, “She’s bright,” or “She understands the material,” but then there would always be this other part about how I was constantly losing worksheets or not turning things in because I couldn’t find them.\n\nI remember rushing through directions, too, like I’d start before I’d fully read what I was supposed to do and then make mistakes that weren’t because I didn’t know the answer. And I talked out of turn a lot. Not in a trying-to-be-rude way, more like the thought came into my head and just came out of my mouth before I stopped it.\n\nMy desk was also… honestly kind of a disaster. I’d have papers shoved everywhere, and then when the teacher asked for an assignment, I’d be digging around while everyone else had theirs out already. So I guess the forgetting and attention stuff didn’t come out of nowhere. It feels like it’s been a pattern since I was a kid, but now there’s less structure around me, so it’s much harder to hide or compensate for.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with forgetting and attention consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It feels like it’s been pretty consistent, just… it looked different when I was younger because adults were kind of holding the structure around me.\n\nLike even in elementary school, I remember teachers writing things along the lines of, “She’s bright,” or that I understood the material, but then in the same breath there’d be comments about me constantly losing worksheets or not having things to turn in because I couldn’t find them. I’d do the work and then somehow it would disappear into my desk or backpack, and then I’d be sitting there panicking while everyone else just handed theirs in.\n\nI also rushed through directions a lot. I’d see the first part of an assignment and just start doing it before I’d actually read the whole thing, and then I’d make these avoidable mistakes. And I talked out of turn a lot, too — not because I was trying to interrupt or be disrespectful, but because the thought would pop into my head and I’d say it before really stopping myself.\n\nAnd my desk was always a mess. Like, not just a little cluttered — it was messy enough that I genuinely couldn’t find assignments when the teacher asked for them. So yes, I don’t think this is something that suddenly appeared in adulthood. It’s more like it’s been there since I was a kid, but now that I’m older, the consequences feel bigger and there’s less built-in help keeping me on track.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, a lot. It’s not always like I’m literally jumping up and running around, but inside it feels really intense. If I have to sit through something like an interview, or a meeting, or even just a movie, I get this pressure in my body where I feel like I need to shift around or get up or do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s almost like I’ll crawl out of my skin if I just sit there completely still. I’ll be changing positions, bouncing my leg, picking at something, fiddling with my sleeves or whatever is nearby. And I’m aware of it, which makes it kind of embarrassing, especially if I’m supposed to look calm and attentive. I can want to sit still, but my body just feels like it’s arguing with me the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with forgetting, attention, and restlessness affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it has. Maybe not always in this dramatic obvious way, but it affects how much people can count on me, and that definitely spills into relationships.\n\nSince I got fired, it’s been especially hard because I’ll tell myself I’m going to follow up after an interview, or send the email, or check back in, and then somehow I miss the window or I forget until it feels too late and embarrassing. And then I avoid it more because I feel ashamed. So even when friends or family are trying to be supportive, I can feel them getting frustrated, like, “Okay, but why didn’t you just do the thing?” And I don’t really have a good answer that doesn’t sound like an excuse.\n\nI also had to step back from a volunteer project I really cared about, which felt awful. It wasn’t that I stopped caring — I cared a lot — but I couldn’t reliably keep up with the basic steps, like remembering follow-ups and staying organized enough to be useful. And that made me feel like I was letting people down.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and it feels like I can’t even reliably manage the normal steps to move my life forward. That’s hard on my family, I think, because they want to help but they also don’t really understand why I keep dropping things. And with friends, I pull back sometimes because I’m embarrassed about where my life is right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact of your forgetfulness and attention issues on your daily life, such as in work, school, or personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. Honestly, that’s the part that feels the most painful right now, because it’s not just “oh, I forgot where I put my keys” or something small. It’s affecting the basic structure of my life.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve been trying to apply and interview for jobs, and I’ll have these moments where I genuinely intend to follow up after an interview — like I know I’m supposed to send the email, I’ll even think about what I want to say — and then I just… don’t do it in time. Or I remember later and it feels too late and awkward, and then I get ashamed and avoid it. And then that obviously doesn’t help me get hired, so I’m still unemployed, and it becomes this cycle where I feel more and more stuck.\n\nIt also affected a volunteer project I really cared about. I didn’t stop caring about it at all, but I had to step back because I couldn’t keep up reliably with the follow-through and organization side of it. That was really upsetting because it made me feel like I was letting people down, and also like I can’t trust myself with things that matter to me.\n\nSo yes, it’s had a significant impact. I feel like I can’t reliably manage the basic steps of moving my life forward right now, even when I really want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. For me it shows up a lot in conversations, especially if I think I know where someone is going with what they’re saying. I’ll jump in before they’ve actually finished the question or the thought, and I’ll answer what I thought they were asking — and then a second later I realize, oh, that wasn’t actually the question, or I just cut them off.\n\nIt happens in interviews too, which is… not great. I’ll be trying so hard to sound engaged and quick, and then I kind of leap ahead. Like they start asking something and my brain fills in the rest, so I start answering before they’re done. Sometimes I can see on their face that I missed what they were really getting at, and then I feel embarrassed and try to correct myself, but by then I’ve already made it awkward.\n\nI don’t usually mean to be rude. It’s more like there’s this pressure to respond immediately, like if I don’t say it right then I’ll lose the thought or miss my chance. But then the consequence is that I interrupt people or answer the wrong thing, and I can tell it affects how I come across.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or considered having ADHD?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I’m here because I’ve wondered if there’s some kind of explanation for why I keep struggling with things that seem like they should be basic, but I haven’t had some clear, formal answer before this.\n\nI guess I’ve considered that maybe there’s something going on, because it feels bigger than just being lazy or careless. But I’m also hesitant to say, “Oh yes, it’s definitely that,” because I don’t want to diagnose myself or sound like I’m just grabbing onto a label. I mostly just know that the problems have been real and they’ve been affecting my life a lot, and I’m trying to understand why.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so intensely focused on something that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I don’t know if I’d describe it as being so intensely focused that the whole world disappears, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I can get absorbed in something sometimes, like anyone can, especially if it’s interesting or if I feel pressure to finish it. But I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern where I regularly lose track of everything around me in a major way. It’s possible I do it and don’t label it that way, but nothing specific is coming to mind that feels important enough to say yes to.\n\nWhen you say “lose track of everything else,” do you mean like forgetting to eat or missing appointments, or more like just being really mentally locked into something for a while?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or restless, even when you're trying to sit still or focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, actually. It’s one of those things I don’t always notice right away because it feels so normal to me, but if I’m in a situation where I’m supposed to just sit still and pay attention, it’s like there’s this pressure building inside my body.\n\nLike in an interview or a meeting, I’ll be trying really hard to look calm and appropriate, but internally I feel like I need to shift positions constantly, or move my hands, or get up, or just do something. Even watching a movie can be hard if I’m expected to just sit there quietly the whole time. It’s not always that I’m anxious exactly — sometimes it’s more physical, like I’m uncomfortable in my own skin.\n\nI’ll cross and uncross my legs, mess with my fingers, adjust how I’m sitting, hold something if I can. If I can’t move at all, it feels almost unbearable, like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. I know that sounds dramatic, but that’s really what it feels like sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention, memory, and restlessness significantly impacted your ability to maintain a job or achieve your educational goals?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… unfortunately, yes, it’s affected that pretty significantly. Since I got fired, it’s felt like I can’t even reliably manage the steps that come after that, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, it’s not just “I don’t have a job right now.” It’s that I’ll have an interview or a possible opportunity, and then I miss the follow-up email, or I forget to respond soon enough, or I get overwhelmed by the basic next step and then too much time passes. And then I feel awful because from the outside it probably looks like I don’t care or I’m not serious, but I do care. I just keep dropping the ball at exactly the moments where I’m supposed to be proving I can handle things.\n\nI also had to step back from a volunteer project that I actually cared about, which was really upsetting. It wasn’t that I stopped caring about it — I really did care — but I couldn’t keep up in a dependable way. And that’s kind of the theme: I feel like I’m not dependable, even when I want to be.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and it feels like I can’t reliably manage the basic things I’m supposed to do to move my life forward. Applying, following up, staying on top of commitments, keeping momentum — those things feel much harder than they seem like they should be. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I have a degree, and I feel like I should be able to function better than this.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’ll jump in before they’ve actually finished the question or the thought, and I’ll answer what I thought they were asking — and then a second later I realize, oh, that wasn’t actually the question, or I just cut them off.\n\nIt happens in interviews too, which is… not great. I’ll be trying so hard to sound engaged and quick, and then I kind of leap ahead. Like they start asking something and my brain fills in the rest, so I start answering before they’re done. Sometimes I can see on their face that I missed what they were really getting at, and then I feel embarrassed and try to correct myself, but by then I’ve already made it awkward.\n\nI don’t usually mean to be rude. It’s more like there’s this pressure to respond immediately, like if I don’t say it right then I’ll lose the thought or miss my chance. But then the consequence is that I interrupt people or answer the wrong thing, and I can tell it affects how I come across.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or considered having ADHD?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I’m here because I’ve wondered if there’s some kind of explanation for why I keep struggling with things that seem like they should be basic, but I haven’t had some clear, formal answer before this.\n\nI guess I’ve considered that maybe there’s something going on, because it feels bigger than just being lazy or careless. But I’m also hesitant to say, “Oh yes, it’s definitely that,” because I don’t want to diagnose myself or sound like I’m just grabbing onto a label. I mostly just know that the problems have been real and they’ve been affecting my life a lot, and I’m trying to understand why.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so intensely focused on something that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I don’t know if I’d describe it as being so intensely focused that the whole world disappears, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I can get absorbed in something sometimes, like anyone can, especially if it’s interesting or if I feel pressure to finish it. But I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern where I regularly lose track of everything around me in a major way. It’s possible I do it and don’t label it that way, but nothing specific is coming to mind that feels important enough to say yes to.\n\nWhen you say “lose track of everything else,” do you mean like forgetting to eat or missing appointments, or more like just being really mentally locked into something for a while?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or restless, even when you're trying to sit still or focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, actually. It’s one of those things I don’t always notice right away because it feels so normal to me, but if I’m in a situation where I’m supposed to just sit still and pay attention, it’s like there’s this pressure building inside my body.\n\nLike in an interview or a meeting, I’ll be trying really hard to look calm and appropriate, but internally I feel like I need to shift positions constantly, or move my hands, or get up, or just do something. Even watching a movie can be hard if I’m expected to just sit there quietly the whole time. It’s not always that I’m anxious exactly — sometimes it’s more physical, like I’m uncomfortable in my own skin.\n\nI’ll cross and uncross my legs, mess with my fingers, adjust how I’m sitting, hold something if I can. If I can’t move at all, it feels almost unbearable, like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. I know that sounds dramatic, but that’s really what it feels like sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention, memory, and restlessness significantly impacted your ability to maintain a job or achieve your educational goals?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… unfortunately, yes, it’s affected that pretty significantly. Since I got fired, it’s felt like I can’t even reliably manage the steps that come after that, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, it’s not just “I don’t have a job right now.” It’s that I’ll have an interview or a possible opportunity, and then I miss the follow-up email, or I forget to respond soon enough, or I get overwhelmed by the basic next step and then too much time passes. And then I feel awful because from the outside it probably looks like I don’t care or I’m not serious, but I do care. I just keep dropping the ball at exactly the moments where I’m supposed to be proving I can handle things.\n\nI also had to step back from a volunteer project that I actually cared about, which was really upsetting. It wasn’t that I stopped caring about it — I really did care — but I couldn’t keep up in a dependable way. And that’s kind of the theme: I feel like I’m not dependable, even when I want to be.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and it feels like I can’t reliably manage the basic things I’m supposed to do to move my life forward. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think they’re both problems, but in a different way. The organization and remembering things probably cause more obvious consequences, like things not getting done. But the restlessness is very significant in the moment, if that makes sense.

Like when I have to sit through an interview, or a meeting, or even just a movie, I get this pressure inside my body where I feel like I need to move — shift around, get up, mess with my hands, something. And if I’m trying to force myself not to, it can feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. It’s not just being a little bored or tapping my foot. It feels physically uncomfortable, like my body is yelling at me to do something.

So I guess the organization and memory stuff might affect my life more obviously, but the restlessness is still a big problem. It takes so much effort to sit still and look normal that I’m using up energy just trying not to move.

  {

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess the main reason is that I feel like I’m not handling basic adult life very well, even things that seem like they should be simple. I keep forgetting important stuff—like paying bills on time, or I’ll mean to reply to an interview email and then somehow days go by and I realize I never answered it. I’ve missed appointments because I either forgot completely or remembered at the wrong time, and errands just kind of… don’t get finished. Like I’ll start the process of doing something and then it disappears from my brain until there’s a consequence.\n\nAnd it’s not just annoying anymore. It’s starting to actually cost me things—opportunities, money, people’s trust, I guess. The interview emails especially have been really upsetting because I’m not working right now, and I know I need to be on top of those, but then I still don’t follow through. So that’s what finally made me think I should come in, because I don’t feel like I can just keep telling myself I’ll do better next time.\n\nDoctor: How have you been feeling emotionally about these situations where you've missed appointments or forgotten important tasks?\n\nPatient: I mean… emotionally, it’s kind of a mix, and I don’t always know how to separate it out neatly. In the moment when I realize I forgot something, there’s usually this really sharp drop in my stomach, like, “Oh no, not again.” Then I get embarrassed, especially if another person was depending on me or waiting for me to respond. I start thinking about how it must look from the outside, like I don’t care or I’m being irresponsible, even though that’s not how it feels from inside my head.\n\nAnd then after that I get really frustrated with myself. Like, I’ll tell myself, “You’re 22, you graduated college, you should be able to answer an email or show up to something you scheduled.” It makes me feel pretty ashamed, honestly. Not every single time in some dramatic way, but it builds up. Each missed thing feels like evidence that I can’t trust myself, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I also get anxious afterward because I know there might be consequences, like someone being upset with me or me losing an opportunity. And then I’ll avoid looking at the email or the missed call for a while because I’m already dreading what it says, which probably makes it worse. But mostly it’s embarrassment, guilt, and this feeling of being really disappointed in myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or pay attention to details in your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that makes me feel the most confused about myself, because it’s not like I’m not trying or I don’t understand what I’m supposed to be doing. Like when I was still doing biotech data work, I would genuinely think I had finished something carefully — like a script or a spreadsheet — and I’d feel pretty confident it was ready. Then later I’d find out I had mislabeled a sample ID, or skipped over some note in the protocol that I should have included, or submitted documentation that had really obvious typos in it.\n\nAnd the worst part is that some of the mistakes were not subtle. It would be something where, looking at it afterward, I’d be like, “How did I not see that?” I could stare at it while I was doing it and somehow still miss the thing right in front of me. It made me really nervous about trusting my own work, because I’d think I had checked it, but apparently my checking didn’t always mean I actually caught things.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems with forgetting and attention start suddenly or have they been gradually getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it started suddenly. It’s more like… I’m only now realizing how long it’s been there, because the consequences are bigger now. When I think back, even in elementary school, teachers used to write comments like, “She’s bright,” or “She understands the material,” but then there would always be this other part about how I was constantly losing worksheets or not turning things in because I couldn’t find them.\n\nI remember rushing through directions, too, like I’d start before I’d fully read what I was supposed to do and then make mistakes that weren’t because I didn’t know the answer. And I talked out of turn a lot. Not in a trying-to-be-rude way, more like the thought came into my head and just came out of my mouth before I stopped it.\n\nMy desk was also… honestly kind of a disaster. I’d have papers shoved everywhere, and then when the teacher asked for an assignment, I’d be digging around while everyone else had theirs out already. So I guess the forgetting and attention stuff didn’t come out of nowhere. It feels like it’s been a pattern since I was a kid, but now there’s less structure around me, so it’s much harder to hide or compensate for.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with forgetting and attention consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It feels like it’s been pretty consistent, just… it looked different when I was younger because adults were kind of holding the structure around me.\n\nLike even in elementary school, I remember teachers writing things along the lines of, “She’s bright,” or that I understood the material, but then in the same breath there’d be comments about me constantly losing worksheets or not having things to turn in because I couldn’t find them. I’d do the work and then somehow it would disappear into my desk or backpack, and then I’d be sitting there panicking while everyone else just handed theirs in.\n\nI also rushed through directions a lot. I’d see the first part of an assignment and just start doing it before I’d actually read the whole thing, and then I’d make these avoidable mistakes. And I talked out of turn a lot, too — not because I was trying to interrupt or be disrespectful, but because the thought would pop into my head and I’d say it before really stopping myself.\n\nAnd my desk was always a mess. Like, not just a little cluttered — it was messy enough that I genuinely couldn’t find assignments when the teacher asked for them. So yes, I don’t think this is something that suddenly appeared in adulthood. It’s more like it’s been there since I was a kid, but now that I’m older, the consequences feel bigger and there’s less built-in help keeping me on track.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, a lot. It’s not always like I’m literally jumping up and running around, but inside it feels really intense. If I have to sit through something like an interview, or a meeting, or even just a movie, I get this pressure in my body where I feel like I need to shift around or get up or do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s almost like I’ll crawl out of my skin if I just sit there completely still. I’ll be changing positions, bouncing my leg, picking at something, fiddling with my sleeves or whatever is nearby. And I’m aware of it, which makes it kind of embarrassing, especially if I’m supposed to look calm and attentive. I can want to sit still, but my body just feels like it’s arguing with me the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with forgetting, attention, and restlessness affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it has. Maybe not always in this dramatic obvious way, but it affects how much people can count on me, and that definitely spills into relationships.\n\nSince I got fired, it’s been especially hard because I’ll tell myself I’m going to follow up after an interview, or send the email, or check back in, and then somehow I miss the window or I forget until it feels too late and embarrassing. And then I avoid it more because I feel ashamed. So even when friends or family are trying to be supportive, I can feel them getting frustrated, like, “Okay, but why didn’t you just do the thing?” And I don’t really have a good answer that doesn’t sound like an excuse.\n\nI also had to step back from a volunteer project I really cared about, which felt awful. It wasn’t that I stopped caring — I cared a lot — but I couldn’t reliably keep up with the basic steps, like remembering follow-ups and staying organized enough to be useful. And that made me feel like I was letting people down.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and it feels like I can’t even reliably manage the normal steps to move my life forward. That’s hard on my family, I think, because they want to help but they also don’t really understand why I keep dropping things. And with friends, I pull back sometimes because I’m embarrassed about where my life is right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact of your forgetfulness and attention issues on your daily life, such as in work, school, or personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. Honestly, that’s the part that feels the most painful right now, because it’s not just “oh, I forgot where I put my keys” or something small. It’s affecting the basic structure of my life.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve been trying to apply and interview for jobs, and I’ll have these moments where I genuinely intend to follow up after an interview — like I know I’m supposed to send the email, I’ll even think about what I want to say — and then I just… don’t do it in time. Or I remember later and it feels too late and awkward, and then I get ashamed and avoid it. And then that obviously doesn’t help me get hired, so I’m still unemployed, and it becomes this cycle where I feel more and more stuck.\n\nIt also affected a volunteer project I really cared about. I didn’t stop caring about it at all, but I had to step back because I couldn’t keep up reliably with the follow-through and organization side of it. That was really upsetting because it made me feel like I was letting people down, and also like I can’t trust myself with things that matter to me.\n\nSo yes, it’s had a significant impact. I feel like I can’t reliably manage the basic steps of moving my life forward right now, even when I really want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. For me it shows up a lot in conversations, especially if I think I know where someone is going with what they’re saying. I’ll jump in before they’ve actually finished the question or the thought, and I’ll answer what I thought they were asking — and then a second later I realize, oh, that wasn’t actually the question, or I just cut them off.\n\nIt happens in interviews too, which is… not great. I’ll be trying so hard to sound engaged and quick, and then I kind of leap ahead. Like they start asking something and my brain fills in the rest, so I start answering before they’re done. Sometimes I can see on their face that I missed what they were really getting at, and then I feel embarrassed and try to correct myself, but by then I’ve already made it awkward.\n\nI don’t usually mean to be rude. It’s more like there’s this pressure to respond immediately, like if I don’t say it right then I’ll lose the thought or miss my chance. But then the consequence is that I interrupt people or answer the wrong thing, and I can tell it affects how I come across.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or considered having ADHD?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I’m here because I’ve wondered if there’s some kind of explanation for why I keep struggling with things that seem like they should be basic, but I haven’t had some clear, formal answer before this.\n\nI guess I’ve considered that maybe there’s something going on, because it feels bigger than just being lazy or careless. But I’m also hesitant to say, “Oh yes, it’s definitely that,” because I don’t want to diagnose myself or sound like I’m just grabbing onto a label. I mostly just know that the problems have been real and they’ve been affecting my life a lot, and I’m trying to understand why.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so intensely focused on something that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I don’t know if I’d describe it as being so intensely focused that the whole world disappears, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I can get absorbed in something sometimes, like anyone can, especially if it’s interesting or if I feel pressure to finish it. But I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern where I regularly lose track of everything around me in a major way. It’s possible I do it and don’t label it that way, but nothing specific is coming to mind that feels important enough to say yes to.\n\nWhen you say “lose track of everything else,” do you mean like forgetting to eat or missing appointments, or more like just being really mentally locked into something for a while?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or restless, even when you're trying to sit still or focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, actually. It’s one of those things I don’t always notice right away because it feels so normal to me, but if I’m in a situation where I’m supposed to just sit still and pay attention, it’s like there’s this pressure building inside my body.\n\nLike in an interview or a meeting, I’ll be trying really hard to look calm and appropriate, but internally I feel like I need to shift positions constantly, or move my hands, or get up, or just do something. Even watching a movie can be hard if I’m expected to just sit there quietly the whole time. It’s not always that I’m anxious exactly — sometimes it’s more physical, like I’m uncomfortable in my own skin.\n\nI’ll cross and uncross my legs, mess with my fingers, adjust how I’m sitting, hold something if I can. If I can’t move at all, it feels almost unbearable, like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. I know that sounds dramatic, but that’s really what it feels like sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention, memory, and restlessness significantly impacted your ability to maintain a job or achieve your educational goals?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… unfortunately, yes, it’s affected that pretty significantly. Since I got fired, it’s felt like I can’t even reliably manage the steps that come after that, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, it’s not just “I don’t have a job right now.” It’s that I’ll have an interview or a possible opportunity, and then I miss the follow-up email, or I forget to respond soon enough, or I get overwhelmed by the basic next step and then too much time passes. And then I feel awful because from the outside it probably looks like I don’t care or I’m not serious, but I do care. I just keep dropping the ball at exactly the moments where I’m supposed to be proving I can handle things.\n\nI also had to step back from a volunteer project that I actually cared about, which was really upsetting. It wasn’t that I stopped caring about it — I really did care — but I couldn’t keep up in a dependable way. And that’s kind of the theme: I feel like I’m not dependable, even when I want to be.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and it feels like I can’t reliably manage the basic things I’m supposed to do to move my life forward. Applying, following up, staying on top of commitments, keeping momentum — those things feel much harder than they seem like they should be. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I have a degree, and I feel like I should be able to function better than this.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and fidgeting are as significant a problem for you as your difficulties with organization and remembering things?\n\nPatient: I think they’re both problems, but in a different way. The organization and remembering things probably cause more obvious consequences, like things not getting done. But the restlessness is very significant in the moment, if that makes sense.\n\nLike when I have to sit through an interview, or a meeting, or even just a movie, I get this pressure inside my body where I feel like I need to move — shift around, get up, mess with my hands, something. And if I’m trying to force myself not to, it can feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. It’s not just being a little bored or tapping my foot. It feels physically uncomfortable, like my body is yelling at me to do something.\n\nSo I guess the organization and memory stuff might affect my life more obviously, but the restlessness is still a big problem. It takes so much effort to sit still and look normal that I’m using up energy just trying not to move.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do think they’re really bothersome, just in a slightly different way. The attention and remembering things are frustrating because they affect what I get done, but the restlessness is more like… I feel trapped in my own body sometimes.

If I’m sitting through an interview, or a meeting, or even something that should be relaxing like a movie, I get this pressure inside me like I need to move. I’ll want to get up, or keep shifting around, or do something with my hands, and if I try not to, it can feel almost unbearable. Like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if I have to stay still one more second.

So yes, it bothers me a lot. It’s not always as visible to other people as forgetting something, but inside it feels very intense.

It feels physically uncomfortable, like my body is yelling at me to do something.\n\nSo I guess the organization and memory stuff might affect my life more obviously, but the restlessness is still a big problem. It takes so much effort to sit still and look normal that I’m using up energy just trying not to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with restlessness and fidgeting are as bothersome to you as your issues with paying attention and remembering things?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think they’re really bothersome, just in a slightly different way. I’ll want to get up, or keep shifting around, or do something with my hands, and if I try not to, it can feel almost unbearable. Like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if I have to stay still one more second.\n\nSo yes, it bothers me a lot. It’s not always as visible to other people as forgetting something, but inside it feels very intense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean, I know what you mean in theory — like getting so absorbed that the rest of the world kind of disappears — but I don’t know if I’d say that happens to me in a clear or consistent way.

I guess there are times where I get really pulled into something, especially if it’s interesting or if there’s pressure around it, but I’m not sure whether that’s the same thing you’re asking about. I don’t really have a specific example coming to mind where I can confidently say, “Yes, I completely forgot everything else around me.” It’s more like… I can get caught up in something, but I don’t know if it’s to that extreme.

So I’d say maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure I’ve noticed it enough to say it’s a major thing for me.

I’d do the work and then somehow it would disappear into my desk or backpack, and then I’d be sitting there panicking while everyone else just handed theirs in.\n\nI also rushed through directions a lot. I’d see the first part of an assignment and just start doing it before I’d actually read the whole thing, and then I’d make these avoidable mistakes. And I talked out of turn a lot, too — not because I was trying to interrupt or be disrespectful, but because the thought would pop into my head and I’d say it before really stopping myself.\n\nAnd my desk was always a mess. Like, not just a little cluttered — it was messy enough that I genuinely couldn’t find assignments when the teacher asked for them. So yes, I don’t think this is something that suddenly appeared in adulthood. It’s more like it’s been there since I was a kid, but now that I’m older, the consequences feel bigger and there’s less built-in help keeping me on track.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, a lot. I’ll be changing positions, bouncing my leg, picking at something, fiddling with my sleeves or whatever is nearby. And I’m aware of it, which makes it kind of embarrassing, especially if I’m supposed to look calm and attentive. I can want to sit still, but my body just feels like it’s arguing with me the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with forgetting, attention, and restlessness affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it has. And then I avoid it more because I feel ashamed. So even when friends or family are trying to be supportive, I can feel them getting frustrated, like, “Okay, but why didn’t you just do the thing?” And I don’t really have a good answer that doesn’t sound like an excuse.\n\nI also had to step back from a volunteer project I really cared about, which felt awful. It feels like it’s been a pattern since I was a kid, but now there’s less structure around me, so it’s much harder to hide or compensate for.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with forgetting and attention consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. It feels like it’s been pretty consistent, just… it looked different when I was younger because adults were kind of holding the structure around me.\n\nLike even in elementary school, I remember teachers writing things along the lines of, “She’s bright,” or that I understood the material, but then in the same breath there’d be comments about me constantly losing worksheets or not having things to turn in because I couldn’t find them. I’d do the work and then somehow it would disappear into my desk or backpack, and then I’d be sitting there panicking while everyone else just handed theirs in.\n\nI also rushed through directions a lot. I’d see the first part of an assignment and just start doing it before I’d actually read the whole thing, and then I’d make these avoidable mistakes. And I talked out of turn a lot, too — not because I was trying to interrupt or be disrespectful, but because the thought would pop into my head and I’d say it before really stopping myself.\n\nAnd my desk was always a mess. Like, not just a little cluttered — it was messy enough that I genuinely couldn’t find assignments when the teacher asked for them. So yes, I don’t think this is something that suddenly appeared in adulthood. It’s more like it’s been there since I was a kid, but now that I’m older, the consequences feel bigger and there’s less built-in help keeping me on track.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, a lot. It’s not always like I’m literally jumping up and running around, but inside it feels really intense. If I have to sit through something like an interview, or a meeting, or even just a movie, I get this pressure in my body where I feel like I need to shift around or get up or do something with my hands.\n\nIt’s hard to explain without sounding dramatic, but it’s almost like I’ll crawl out of my skin if I just sit there completely still. I’ll be changing positions, bouncing my leg, picking at something, fiddling with my sleeves or whatever is nearby. And I’m aware of it, which makes it kind of embarrassing, especially if I’m supposed to look calm and attentive. I can want to sit still, but my body just feels like it’s arguing with me the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with forgetting, attention, and restlessness affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it has. Maybe not always in this dramatic obvious way, but it affects how much people can count on me, and that definitely spills into relationships.\n\nSince I got fired, it’s been especially hard because I’ll tell myself I’m going to follow up after an interview, or send the email, or check back in, and then somehow I miss the window or I forget until it feels too late and embarrassing. And then I avoid it more because I feel ashamed. So even when friends or family are trying to be supportive, I can feel them getting frustrated, like, “Okay, but why didn’t you just do the thing?” And I don’t really have a good answer that doesn’t sound like an excuse.\n\nI also had to step back from a volunteer project I really cared about, which felt awful. It wasn’t that I stopped caring — I cared a lot — but I couldn’t reliably keep up with the basic steps, like remembering follow-ups and staying organized enough to be useful. And that made me feel like I was letting people down.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and it feels like I can’t even reliably manage the normal steps to move my life forward. That’s hard on my family, I think, because they want to help but they also don’t really understand why I keep dropping things. And with friends, I pull back sometimes because I’m embarrassed about where my life is right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact of your forgetfulness and attention issues on your daily life, such as in work, school, or personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. Honestly, that’s the part that feels the most painful right now, because it’s not just “oh, I forgot where I put my keys” or something small. It’s affecting the basic structure of my life.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve been trying to apply and interview for jobs, and I’ll have these moments where I genuinely intend to follow up after an interview — like I know I’m supposed to send the email, I’ll even think about what I want to say — and then I just… don’t do it in time. Or I remember later and it feels too late and awkward, and then I get ashamed and avoid it. And then that obviously doesn’t help me get hired, so I’m still unemployed, and it becomes this cycle where I feel more and more stuck.\n\nIt also affected a volunteer project I really cared about. I didn’t stop caring about it at all, but I had to step back because I couldn’t keep up reliably with the follow-through and organization side of it. That was really upsetting because it made me feel like I was letting people down, and also like I can’t trust myself with things that matter to me.\n\nSo yes, it’s had a significant impact. I feel like I can’t reliably manage the basic steps of moving my life forward right now, even when I really want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. For me it shows up a lot in conversations, especially if I think I know where someone is going with what they’re saying. I’ll jump in before they’ve actually finished the question or the thought, and I’ll answer what I thought they were asking — and then a second later I realize, oh, that wasn’t actually the question, or I just cut them off.\n\nIt happens in interviews too, which is… not great. I’ll be trying so hard to sound engaged and quick, and then I kind of leap ahead. Like they start asking something and my brain fills in the rest, so I start answering before they’re done. Sometimes I can see on their face that I missed what they were really getting at, and then I feel embarrassed and try to correct myself, but by then I’ve already made it awkward.\n\nI don’t usually mean to be rude. It’s more like there’s this pressure to respond immediately, like if I don’t say it right then I’ll lose the thought or miss my chance. But then the consequence is that I interrupt people or answer the wrong thing, and I can tell it affects how I come across.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with or considered having ADHD?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I’m here because I’ve wondered if there’s some kind of explanation for why I keep struggling with things that seem like they should be basic, but I haven’t had some clear, formal answer before this.\n\nI guess I’ve considered that maybe there’s something going on, because it feels bigger than just being lazy or careless. But I’m also hesitant to say, “Oh yes, it’s definitely that,” because I don’t want to diagnose myself or sound like I’m just grabbing onto a label. I mostly just know that the problems have been real and they’ve been affecting my life a lot, and I’m trying to understand why.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so intensely focused on something that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I don’t know if I’d describe it as being so intensely focused that the whole world disappears, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I can get absorbed in something sometimes, like anyone can, especially if it’s interesting or if I feel pressure to finish it. But I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern where I regularly lose track of everything around me in a major way. It’s possible I do it and don’t label it that way, but nothing specific is coming to mind that feels important enough to say yes to.\n\nWhen you say “lose track of everything else,” do you mean like forgetting to eat or missing appointments, or more like just being really mentally locked into something for a while?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or restless, even when you're trying to sit still or focus on something?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much, actually. It’s one of those things I don’t always notice right away because it feels so normal to me, but if I’m in a situation where I’m supposed to just sit still and pay attention, it’s like there’s this pressure building inside my body.\n\nLike in an interview or a meeting, I’ll be trying really hard to look calm and appropriate, but internally I feel like I need to shift positions constantly, or move my hands, or get up, or just do something. Even watching a movie can be hard if I’m expected to just sit there quietly the whole time. It’s not always that I’m anxious exactly — sometimes it’s more physical, like I’m uncomfortable in my own skin.\n\nI’ll cross and uncross my legs, mess with my fingers, adjust how I’m sitting, hold something if I can. If I can’t move at all, it feels almost unbearable, like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. I know that sounds dramatic, but that’s really what it feels like sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention, memory, and restlessness significantly impacted your ability to maintain a job or achieve your educational goals?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… unfortunately, yes, it’s affected that pretty significantly. Since I got fired, it’s felt like I can’t even reliably manage the steps that come after that, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, it’s not just “I don’t have a job right now.” It’s that I’ll have an interview or a possible opportunity, and then I miss the follow-up email, or I forget to respond soon enough, or I get overwhelmed by the basic next step and then too much time passes. And then I feel awful because from the outside it probably looks like I don’t care or I’m not serious, but I do care. It feels physically uncomfortable, like my body is yelling at me to do something.\n\nSo I guess the organization and memory stuff might affect my life more obviously, but the restlessness is still a big problem. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely. That happens a lot, and it’s honestly one of the things that makes me feel the most frustrated with myself.

Like, I’ll sit down with a very specific intention — for example, to update my resume or work through a coding practice problem — and I’ll think, okay, I’m doing this now, this is the task. And then something tiny will happen, like a text notification, or someone talking outside my room, or even just a random thought like, “Oh, I should check that other thing,” and suddenly I’m completely pulled away from what I was doing.

The weird part is I don’t always notice it right away. It’s not always like I consciously decide, “I’m going to stop this and do something else.” It’s more like my mind gets grabbed by something, and then a while later I realize I’ve lost the thread of the original task. And if there were instructions or steps I was supposed to follow, I’ll realize I missed part of them or forgot what I was supposed to do next.

So yes, I would say my mind is elsewhere pretty often, even when I’m trying to pay attention. It can feel like I’m present physically, but my attention just slips away before I catch it.
